The leading engines also agreed closely with one another, and the lowest pairwise κ values all involved at least one of the three lowest-ranked engines (Fig. 4c). Across all 11 engines, pairwise κ ranged from 0.43 to 0.97, with a median of 0.80. On the 250 ground-truthpositive charts, ten of the 11 engines reached 95.2% to 99.6% sensitivity, compared with 80.8% for the AUD diagnosis (Fig. 4d). On the 173 ground-truth-negative charts, specificity ranged from 47.4% to 95.4%, against 90.2% for the AUD diagnosis, with the lowest values for the three engines that rarely returned AUD-.

MedDx maintained its agreement with the ground truth across the skill and its four variations, with κ ranging from 0.90 to 0.93 (Extended Data Fig. 5). The variations were the same skill at temperatures 0.3 and 0.7, and paraphrased skill A and paraphrased skill B at temperature 0. Paraphrased skill B produced the largest paired change from the skill, -0.018 (95% paired bootstrap interval -0.042 to +0.004). All four paired intervals included zero, and each skill variation changed the three-class placement of 10 to 13 of the 485 charts. On the 186 charts hepatologist B also read, MedDx kept the same class under each variation on 96.8 to 97.8% of charts (κ 0.94 to 0.96; Fig. 4e). On those charts, hepatologists A and B agreed on 91.9% (κ 0.87), and MedDx agreed with either hepatologist on 89.2 to 89.8% (κ 0.82).

MedDx's supporting quotations can be checked against the chart: 447 of the 455 are verbatim in the source note (98.2%; 95% CI 96.6-99.2) (Fig. 4f). Of the 485 quotation fields, 433 match one passage word for word and 14 join non-adjacent passages with an ellipsis. The other eight differ from the note in formatting, and manual review found no hallucinated content in any of them. The remaining 30 fields carry no quotation because MedDx reported that the chart held nothing about alcohol, and the ground truth was indeterminate for all 30.

MedDx identified at least one rule finding in 230 of the 250 ground-truth-positive patients, most often an explicit diagnosis of AUD in the notes (201 patients). Among those 250 patients, it most often cited the history of present illness (73%), the discharge diagnosis (58%) and the problem list (58%). Among the 173 ground-truth-negative patients, it most often cited the social history (90%) and the structured field (46%) (Fig. 4g; Extended Data Fig. 6). charts), Wilson 95% CI, for MedDx under the skill and its four variations (filled) and the other ten engines (open); the blue line and band are the AUD diagnosis. e, Agreement between two readings of the same chart on the 186 charts hepatologist B read: hepatologists A and B, MedDx against each hepatologist, and MedDx against itself under two temperatures and two paraphrases of the skill. f, Audit of the quotations of the 485 classifications against the notes (Methods). g, The ten note types or sections MedDx cited most often: the share of ground-truth AUD-(green, n = 173) and AUD+ (plum, n = 250) patients whose classification cited each. The structured field is a form field inside the note, such as the social history box.

Discussion

Our results show that MedDx, a language model reading clinical notes, identifies AUD in patients with cirrhosis in close agreement with hepatologists and more accurately than the ICD diagnosis code for AUD. We tested it against hepatologists' review of 485 charts from a statewide registry of 14,406 patients with cirrhosis. Its agreement with the hepatologists was similar across the hospital systems studied, and its sensitivity varied little across patient groups. Weighting the hepatologists' classifications to the registry, we estimate that most of its patients with AUD carried no diagnosis code for AUD at the first cirrhosis diagnosis. These results add the clinical notes as a source that work now relying on diagnosis codes can use.

MedDx kept its accuracy across hospital systems, which we attribute to two properties. First, it reads what clinicians wrote. A diagnosis code answers to billing and to local documentation practice 12, 31 , whereas notes are written by clinicians for clinicians, and the gain in sensitivity from reading them was greatest at the safety-net hospital, where the diagnosis code's sensitivity was lowest. Second, it is training-free. Phenotypes and models fitted at one institution often lose accuracy at another [32] [33] [34] , and both supervised models, trained at the academic health system, the best-resourced of the three, lost specificity when applied unchanged elsewhere. MedDx has no training step, so there is nothing to refit when it moves to another hospital.

Another registry can run MedDx as it stands and check what it returns. The skill states the hepatologists' rule in plain language and is released in full (Supplementary Note 3; Code availability), every engine we tested has open weights, and the leading engines agreed closely with one another, which gives registries a choice among tested engines. Each classification names its rule findings, the note type or section it drew on and a verbatim quotation, so a clinician can check it against the chart, which neither a diagnosis code nor a model score allows. A fabricated quotation, a known failure of language models 35, 36 , would show as text absent from the note. Where the notes held nothing about alcohol, MedDx returned indeterminate, as the hepatologists did, and the indeterminate class shows a user where the notes leave MedDx little to read 37.

Our findings extend earlier work showing that diagnosis codes miss AUD confirmed by chart review and that clinical notes can identify the patients the codes miss 7, 8, 22 . In cirrhosis, earlier studies compared alcohol-related codes with the chart for alcoholassociated liver disease 38 and found unhealthy alcohol use on screening 39 . Hepatologist adjudication in a population-based cirrhosis registry allowed us to estimate whom a cohort defined by these codes would include. At the first cirrhosis diagnosis, requiring a diagnosis code for AUD would exclude three-fifths of registry patients with AUD, and accepting any alcohol-related diagnosis code would still exclude two-fifths.

Among registry patients with AUD, uptake of AUD-specific medication was lowest in those with no alcohol-related diagnosis code at the first cirrhosis diagnosis. Low uptake is established nationally 40 and among patients with cirrhosis and a diagnosis code for AUD 3 . Within one year of an alcohol-associated cirrhosis diagnosis, 0.8% of commercially insured patients and 9.7% of veterans with high-risk drinking started an AUD medication 4,41 , and patients with AUD and cirrhosis had lower odds of receiving one than patients with AUD and no liver disease, an adjusted odds ratio of 0.22 42 . The estimates in cirrhosis start from a recorded alcohol-related diagnosis code. Our results extend them to patients whom a cohort defined by alcohol-related diagnosis codes would miss at the first cirrhosis diagnosis.

This study has limitations. First, disagreement between MedDx and hepatologist A was one reason a chart received a second reading, so agreement with the ground truth may be optimistic. Second, the registry is built on a single statewide health information exchange and all its patients have cirrhosis, so MedDx remains to be validated in other registries and conditions. Third, some patient groups were small, and comparisons between groups should be repeated in larger samples 43, 44.

Our results underline that the AUD the structured record under-documents in cirrhosis is largely written in the notes, where MedDx reads it with similar accuracy across hospital systems. A prospective study could test whether showing clinicians the chart evidence that MedDx identifies changes who receives treatment.

Methods

Study design, registry and study cohort

This was a retrospective diagnostic accuracy study using clinical notes from the statewide health information exchange on which the registry is built (the Indiana Network for Patient Care 27 ). Structured data came from the registry's structured record, and the 14,406 patients with a K70, K74 or ICD-9 571 code in it entered the analysis. Time zero (t0) is the date of the first cirrhosis diagnosis, the first code specific to cirrhosis in the record (ICD-10 K70.3, K71.7 or K74.3 to K74.6; ICD-9 571.2, 571.5 or 571.6), or the first K70, K74 or 571 code for the three patients without one, and is independent of AUD diagnosis status. The record starts on 1 January 2015, the lower bound on t0: a patient whose first cirrhosis code fell before that date enters with t0 in early 2015. Observed t0 runs from January 2015 to February 2023, and 99.8% of the registry enters by the end of 2020.

The study cohort comprised the 485 patients drawn by stratified sampling from the ICR 26 . The charts were drawn within strata of AUD diagnosis status at any time in the structured record, in roughly equal numbers from the 4,210 registry patients with an AUD diagnosis and the 10,196 without, and unequally across hospital types (Registry estimate). Extended Data Fig. 1 gives the flow of patients from the registry to the analyzed set.

Clinical notes

For each patient in the study cohort, the notes were every note retrieved in a window of 18 months either side of that patient's study-cohort index date. The study-cohort index date is the first recorded AUD diagnosis for the 231 patients sampled as having an AUD diagnosis whose record includes one and the first recorded cirrhosis diagnosis for the remaining 254. For patients without an AUD diagnosis, the hepatologists judged the cirrhosis diagnosis the point in the record most likely to include a discussion of alcohol. The study-cohort index date anchors the note window and is a different quantity from the first cirrhosis diagnosis of the registry analysis (t0).

The notes came from three hospital systems: an academic health system (224 patients), a safety-net hospital (57), and other contributing hospitals (204). Each patient in the study cohort was assigned to the hospital system that wrote most of that patient's delivered notes, using the data provider's report on the delivered notes.

MedDx

MedDx reads each patient's notes under the skill and returns one of six grades, which collapse to the three classes used throughout: AUD+, AUD-and indeterminate (Supplementary Table 1). It has three components, the skill, the harness and the engine.

The skill.

The skill is an eight-block extraction and classification instruction, obtained by structured elicitation of hepatologist A's own chart-review reasoning, and is printed in full in Supplementary Note 3. The eight blocks state four rule findings, each sufficient for AUD+, and require with every classification the rule findings, the note type or section cited and a verbatim quotation. The skill was completed before any MedDx output was compared with a ground-truth classification. Every chart was processed once under the skill, and the same skill was used for every hospital system.

The harness.

The harness is the fixed program that runs the skill on the engine, one patient at a time. It passes the skill and the patient's notes, concatenated into one input, to the engine and returns the classification in a fixed set of fields: one of the six grades, the rule findings, the note type or section, the supporting quotation and a confidence. When a patient's notes exceed the engine's input limit, the harness divides them into parts, classifies each part and keeps the strongest of the five grades from definitely no to definitely yes that any part received. Not enough data stands only when every part received it. The quotation, note type or section and confidence are those of the part with the strongest grade, and the rule findings are pooled across parts. Whether each quotation is verbatim in the notes was assessed afterwards (Quotation check).

The engine.

The engine is the open-weight language model that reads the notes under the skill. Eleven engines were evaluated on the same 485 charts under the same skill and harness, and their repositories, revisions, release dates, sizes and numeric formats are in Supplementary Table 14. The eleven span eight developers, parameter counts from 21.5B to 753B and release dates from November 2024 to July 2026. Each was the current instruction-tuned release of its line at the time of evaluation. Qwen3.6-27B is the primary engine (Engines and skill variations).

Ground truth

Chart review was performed by four hepatologists at Indiana University School of Medicine. Hepatologists A and B are hepatology fellows, hepatologist C is Associate Professor of Medicine and hepatologist D is Professor of Medicine. Hepatologist A read every chart, hepatologist B read a random subsample, hepatologist C gave second readings and hepatologist D adjudicated. All four read blind to all MedDx output.

The classification rule.

The skill states the same classification rule the hepatologists used, having been elicited from the hepatologist who wrote their abstraction form, so agreement between MedDx and a hepatologist measures how reliably one rule is executed on a chart. Each of four rule findings is sufficient for definitely yes: alcohol-associated cirrhosis named as such, an explicit diagnosis of alcohol use disorder, dependence or abuse, an AUDIT score of 8 or more or a CAGE score of 2 or more, and documented withdrawal or its management. Current sobriety leaves the grade unchanged. Alcohol-attributed disease other than cirrhosis reaches probably yes on its own. Where a structured field and the narrative conflict, the narrative governs, and a structured field stands alone only where no narrative mentions alcohol. The hepatologists' abstraction form searched each chart under eight headings: diagnosis and problem-list terms, drinking quantity and pattern, the AUDIT and CAGE instruments, withdrawal and its management, alcohol-attributed disease, treatment and recovery, supportive care, and social and legal circumstances.

Second reading and adjudication.

Hepatologist A read every chart in the study cohort and returned one of the six grades. A chart went on to a second reading by hepatologist C for either of two reasons (Extended Data Fig. 2). The first was disagreement with MedDx. MedDx was run with three engines, Qwen3.6-27B, GPT-OSS-120B and GLM-5.2, and a chart went to a second reading when its classification under any one of the three differed from hepatologist A's at the three-class level, which selected 57 charts. The second was disagreement between two hepatologists. Hepatologist B read a random subsample of 186 charts, blind to hepatologist A's classifications, and took no part in adjudication. Hepatologists A and B differed on 15 charts at the three-class level. Seven were already among the 57 selected for the first reason, and the remaining 8 went to a second reading for this reason alone.

Hepatologist C read 65 charts: 50 for the first reason alone, 8 for the second alone and 7 for both. Hepatologist D adjudicated the 36 on which hepatologist C and hepatologist A differed in class. The ground truth is hepatologist D's classification on the charts he read, hepatologist C's on the other charts with a second reading and hepatologist A's on the charts with a single reading, whichever reason led to the second reading. Of the 485 classifications, 420 are hepatologist A's, 29 are hepatologist C's and 36 are hepatologist D's. The share of charts each hepatologist classified and the agreement between hepatologists are in Supplementary Table 3.

Verification-independent ground truth.

The first reason for a second reading depends on MedDx output, so charts were verified at different intensities according to what MedDx returned (differential verification 45 ). A verification-independent ground truth was therefore built from the second reason alone, over the 186 charts hepatologist B read. It takes hepatologist A's classification where hepatologists A and B agree and the classification of hepatologist C or D where they differ, so its charts were selected without reference to MedDx. Of the 186, 161 have an AUD+ or AUD-ground truth, 96 AUD+ and 65 AUD-, and the remaining 25 are indeterminate and contribute no scored comparison. Registry-weighted specificity against it is reported in the Results, under weights formed over the charts that contribute to it.

Registry estimate

Structured diagnoses were grouped by code set. An AUD diagnosis is an ICD code for AUD (F10.x, ICD-9 303.x or 305.0x). An alcohol-related diagnosis other than AUD is any alcoholrelated code that is not an AUD code, including alcohol-associated liver disease (K70, ICD-9 571.0-571.3). No alcohol-related diagnosis is the absence of any code in the set. At the first cirrhosis diagnosis (t0), each patient falls into exactly one of three strata defined by these categories. ICD-9 codes are included because the record spans the October 2015 transition, and a K70-only definition of alcohol-associated liver disease is confounded with calendar time. Code lists are provided in Supplementary Table 15.

The registry estimate of the number of patients with AUD is the ground truth, post-stratified to the registry. The 423 reviewed charts with an AUD+ or AUD-ground truth are included, and the 62 indeterminate charts are described and carry no weight (Extended Data Fig. 3). Every registry patient carries a hospital type, determined by the sites of the patient's inpatient and emergency visits: safety-net hospital if any visit was there; academic health system if any visit was there; otherwise, other hospitals. Every registry patient also carries a whole-record diagnosis status: an AUD diagnosis at any time, an alcohol-related diagnosis other than AUD, or neither. The nine cells of hospital type and diagnosis status contain the 14,406 registry patients and the 423 reviewed charts with an AUD+ or AUDground truth (Extended Data Fig. 4; Supplementary Table 5).

Within each cell, the share with AUD is the share of its AUD+ or AUD-charts whose ground truth is AUD+, and the cell's patients with AUD are that share multiplied by its registry patients. The registry estimate is the sum over the nine cells, totaling 6,223 patients. Equivalently, each AUD+ or AUD-chart carries the weight of the registry estimate, the registry patients of its hospital type and whole-record diagnosis status, over the AUD+ or AUD-charts in that cell that contribute to the estimate 46 . Any count is then the weighted number of AUD+ charts: the count within a stratum at t0, a hospital type, or a patient group is the weighted number of AUD+ charts among the reviewed charts in that stratum, type, or group. The same weights, applied to MedDx's classifications of the same charts, yield the MedDx projection in Fig. 2b.

Intervals are percentile bootstraps over the AUD+ or AUD-charts, resampled within the cells, with 2,000 resamples. Weights are set by cell, and a stratum at t0 cuts across cells, so a stratum's interval can exceed its registry size. The upper bound of the other alcoholrelated diagnosis stratum exceeds its registry size, and that of the AUD diagnosis stratum does as well, so the AUD diagnosis stratum's share is reported as a point.

Sensitivity analyses and assumptions.

Five specifications vary the registry estimate (Supplementary Table 16). The indeterminate charts were counted as AUD-(lower bound) and AUD+ (upper bound), with a tipping-point series between the lower bound and the primary estimate. The cells were reduced to hospital type and AUD diagnosis alone, resulting in six cells. The hospital type of a reviewed chart was taken from the source of its notes rather than its visits, while the registry retained the visit-based type.

The registry estimate rests on two assumptions. First, within a cell defined by hospital type and whole-record diagnosis status, entry into the study cohort and the AUD+ or AUDground truth are independent of AUD status. Second, the ground truth measures the target condition, defined as lifetime AUD, regardless of whether AUD is present at t0.

Agreement and accuracy

The target condition is chart-documented lifetime AUD. Performance against the ground truth was measured by agreement under the primary definition below and, on the threeclass collapse, by sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value (PPV) and negative predictive value (NPV). Patients whose ground-truth classification is indeterminate are excluded from sensitivity and specificity. An indeterminate output on a patient with an AUD+ or AUD-ground truth stays in the denominator, so an indeterminate MedDx output on a ground-truth-positive patient counts as a patient MedDx did not identify. The intervals are described under Statistical analysis.

The primary agreement statistic is unweighted κ 47 on the three classes, AUD+, AUD-and indeterminate. The three classes are the decision the classification supports, and indeterminate is an output state with no position between the two directions, so the classes carry no distance weights. A chart is excluded when either of the two classifications compared is not enough data, so the primary denominator is 428 of the 485 charts and the indeterminate class of the primary κ contains uncertain alone. Counting not enough data in that class over all 485 charts gives 0.93 (Supplementary Table 17), and agreement within each of MedDx's six grades is in Supplementary Table 2. The three-class collapse used for the descriptive counts in Fig. 1c pools uncertain and not enough data, on a different denominator from that of the κ. A weighted κ on the full six-grade scale requires a position for not enough data, which has no natural one. Across the six positions it can occupy, the same comparison gives 0.89 to 0.92. Six definitions are reported beside the primary one in Supplementary Table 17, for MedDx against the ground truth and for both comparisons against hepatologist B. They are the same three classes with not enough data kept as indeterminate, with linear weights 48 and with Gwet's AC1 49 , the two directional classes restricted to charts on which both classifications were directional, the five classes that remain when not enough data is dropped and the scale is ordinal, and the six-grade metric under both orderings. Weights on the six-grade κ are linear (Supplementary Table 17). The conclusions of the agreement analysis hold under all six. The two comparisons against hepatologist B use first-pass classifications, before adjudication. Agreement between hepatologists A and B measures the reproducibility of the reading, and MedDx against hepatologist B on the same charts is reported beside it under the same definitions (Supplementary Table 17).

Estimates are reported on the study cohort and weighted to the registry with the weights of the registry estimate (Registry estimate). Sensitivity, specificity and Youden's J 29 are reported unweighted and weighted, and the weighted form describes the registry. The share of charts with a directional classification, and its complement the indeterminate rate, are reported beside accuracy on the same denominator. Predictive values are reported for the registry. The positive predictive value is the weighted share of the charts classified AUD+ whose ground truth is AUD+, and the negative predictive value the weighted share of those classified AUD-whose ground truth is AUD-, with the bootstrap of the registry estimate. Fig. 1f draws both against prevalence from the weighted rates of AUD+ and AUD-classification among ground-truth-positive and ground-truth-negative charts, an indeterminate output counting as neither.

Comparators

The comparator set spans both data sources. Two read the structured record, a code rule and a supervised model, and two read the same notes MedDx read, both supervised models. Each was scored against the same ground truth on the same AUD+ or AUDcharts. Variants of each and full metrics are in Supplementary Tables 18 and 19.

The rule comparator flags any alcohol-related diagnosis, a code set wider than F10.x and given in Supplementary Table 15. A variant adds alcohol-associated liver disease to the AUD diagnosis. The supervised comparator is a gradient-boosted tree over age, sex, race, ethnicity, the 19 Charlson conditions, the comorbidity index, encounter and note counts, and two alcohol-code flags. Its features exclude hospital system. It was fitted with five-fold outer and three-fold inner cross-validation and is reported out of fold, both with and without the AUD diagnosis among its features. The version without the AUD diagnosis is the principal supervised structured comparator, because the patients of interest are those the diagnosis does not identify.

Two comparators read the same notes as MedDx. The first is a TF-IDF logistic regression: an L2-regularized logistic regression over word and bigram counts with sublinear term frequency and inverse document frequency, fitted on each patient's concatenated notes. The second is Bio_ClinicalBERT 30 , a BERT encoder pretrained on clinical notes and finetuned for this classification. A patient's concatenated notes exceed the encoder's 512token input, so they were divided into non-overlapping 512-token windows, each of which inherits the patient's ground-truth classification. Training used at most 32 windows per patient, taken at even spacing through the notes, which limits the weight of a long chart in the loss. At inference every window was scored and the patient's score is the mean over its windows.

Both text comparators used the same nested cross-validation as the structured model. Every fold, outer and inner, was drawn over patients and the windows were cut after the split, so all of a patient's text lies on one side of every split. The operating threshold for every supervised comparator is the quantile of the training fold's own score distribution taken at that training fold's ground-truth-positive prevalence, so the threshold uses training-fold classifications only.

Transfer across hospital systems.

The gradient-boosted model with the AUD diagnosis among its inputs and the TF-IDF logistic regression were also fitted on the AUD+ or AUD-charts of the academic health system alone (191 charts), with the same features, grids and nested cross-validation as above. Each was then applied unchanged and scored on the safety-net hospital (56 charts) and the other hospitals (176). The classification threshold was the quantile of the out-offold scores at the academic health system's ground-truth prevalence, carried unchanged. Sensitivity and specificity by system have Wilson 95% CI. The academic-system values are the 5-fold out-of-fold estimates on the training charts (Fig. 3e; Supplementary Table 8).

AUD-specific medication

The outcome is the first recorded receipt, on or after the first cirrhosis diagnosis (t0), of any of the three medications approved for AUD in the United States: naltrexone (standard concept 1714319), acamprosate (19043959) or disulfiram (735850), with descendants. Methylnaltrexone (909841), a peripheral opioid antagonist, was excluded, as were the morphine-naltrexone and bupropion-naltrexone combination products, which are not AUD treatment. Gabapentin, baclofen and topiramate are not counted, because their recorded use before t0 does not follow the alcohol-related diagnoses (Supplementary Fig. 4). A patient with receipt before t0 is a prior user, counted as treated at t0 and outside the time-to-event analysis. Every other patient is a new user, followed on a time axis from t0.

Among patients with AUD in a stratum at t0, the share on an AUD-specific drug by year t is the stratum's prior users plus its new users who first received one within t years of t0, over the registry estimate of patients with AUD in the stratum. The new users treated by year t are the stratum's new users times the Aalen-Johansen cumulative incidence of a first AUD-specific drug, with death as a competing event, from t0 to the first of the drug, death, or the end of the record on 10 July 2023 (Extended Data Fig. 7a). The prior users and the study-cohort charts of each stratum are in Extended Data Fig. 7b,c. The gap between strata is the difference in shares, at t0 and at five years, and the ratio is the ratio of the five-year shares (Extended Data Table 2). Intervals are percentile bootstraps over 500 paired resamples, the AUD+ or AUD-charts within cells for the denominator and the stratum's new users for the cumulative incidence.

Deaths are recorded whether or not a patient is still seen, so follow-up was censored at the end of the record. A patient no longer seen counts as untreated and at risk, which lowers the shares. In the sensitivity analysis follow-up was censored at each patient's last record, which moves the shares in the other direction (Extended Data Table 2). The shares count every recipient of an AUD-specific drug as a patient with AUD, the third assumption of the registry-scale analysis, on the ground that a patient with cirrhosis who receives one of these three drugs has the disorder.

Patient groups and hospital systems

Performance was computed within each hospital system with the measures above, with the primary κ for agreement (Agreement and accuracy). The paired gain reported for a hospital system is MedDx's sensitivity there minus the AUD diagnosis's on the same charts, in percentage points, with a percentile bootstrap interval over the patients of that system. Agreement was also computed within three calendar periods of the first cirrhosis diagnosis, 2015-16, 2017-18 and 2019 onward (Supplementary Fig. 1).

Differences between patient groups were assessed at two scales, the reviewed charts and the registry, each with the variables recorded for every patient at that scale. At chart scale, six variables supply 18 groups: hospital system, sex, race, age group, urban-rural classification and neighborhood deprivation quintile. At registry scale, five variables supply 15: age group, sex, race, Charlson index and neighborhood deprivation quintile. Hospital system and urban-rural classification were linked for the reviewed charts alone, and the Charlson index is reported at registry scale only. Age is grouped as under 50, 50 to 64 and 65 and over. A patient with no recorded value of a variable is in none of that variable's groups.

Neighborhood deprivation is the Social Deprivation Index of the Robert Graham Center, built on the 2015-2019 American Community Survey and published for each ZIP code tabulation area. The five-digit ZIP recorded for each patient was matched to the tabulation area of the same five digits. The published score is a population-weighted national percentile, so each 20-point band contains one fifth of the United States population and defines a quintile without reference to these data. The index was linked for 14,207 of the 14,406 registry patients and 481 of the 485 reviewed charts. Comorbidity burden used the Charlson index in the Glasheen specification 50 with the National Cancer Institute code lists 51 , applying the hierarchy rules under which a severe condition supersedes its milder form and requiring co-occurring HIV and an opportunistic infection for the AIDS component. The two liver components were excluded because every registry patient has cirrhosis.

At chart scale, the quantity is the difference in sensitivity and in specificity between a group and all other groups of its variable, in percentage points, computed for the AUD diagnosis and for each model on the same charts, with zero as no difference and a 4,000sample bootstrap. The reference band is 5 points, the reporting threshold, and the intervals are given beside it.

Two summaries of the 18 chart-scale differences are reported beside the counts of groups outside the band, for the AUD diagnosis and for each model. The span on a metric is the largest of the 18 differences minus the smallest. The same 18 groups are also placed as operating points in a plane whose axes are the group's false positive rate and its sensitivity, both in percentage points, and the area of the convex hull of the 18 points is reported in square points. The same estimator on the same 18 groups was applied to the 11 engines of Fig. 4b and to the skill with its four variations of Extended Data Fig. 5.

At registry scale, the quantity is the sensitivity of the AUD diagnosis by t0 among registry patients estimated to have AUD, which uses the weights of the registry estimate (Registry estimate) within the group. For a group, the estimated number of patients with AUD is the weighted number of AUD+ charts among its reviewed charts. Sensitivity is the weighted share of them in the stratum that carries an AUD diagnosis at t0, and the remainder are the patients with AUD in the group who carry no AUD diagnosis at t0. The reviewed charts alone cannot supply this quantity because they were drawn stratified on diagnosis status. Confidence intervals are the percentile bootstrap of the registry estimate, 2,000 resamples of the AUD+ or AUD-charts within cells. Sensitivity in a group is also expressed as a ratio to the same quantity in all other groups of that variable, with one as no difference. Intervals for the ratio are 2,000-sample percentile bootstrap intervals over the AUD+ or AUDcharts, resampled within the cells of the weights. The band on that axis is the four-fifths rule, 0.8 to 1.25, whose edges multiply to one.

Indeterminate rate by group.

The rate of indeterminate classification was stratified separately from accuracy, on a denominator of every chart in the group, including the charts whose ground-truth classification is itself indeterminate. Seven variables were used, each taken from the structured record or from the patient's ZIP: hospital system, sex, race, age group, decompensation at the first cirrhosis diagnosis, first cirrhosis diagnosis before or from March 2020, and urban-rural classification. Both indeterminate grades count for MedDx, matching the single indeterminate class of the ground truth, and the same quantity was computed for the hepatologists on the same charts. Intervals are Wilson intervals. One of the 485 charts has no urban-rural classification, so each rate is computed on the charts assigned to a level of its variable. Every group is in Supplementary Table 9.

Engines and skill variations

Qwen3.6-27B is the primary engine because it agreed most closely with the ground truth on these 485 charts. The three engines with the highest agreement are marked as recommended in Fig. 4b, and GPT-OSS-20B and every engine ranked below it are marked as not recommended. Agreement between every pair of engines was computed under the primary definition, with the ground truth entered as one more row (Fig. 4c), and rows and columns keep the order of Fig. 4b. Patients were resampled 2,000 times and every engine's agreement recomputed in each resample, recording how often each engine came first and the 2.5th to 97.5th percentile of its rank (Supplementary Table 13).

The skill was run at temperature 0. Four variations were run: the same skill at temperature 0.3 and at temperature 0.7, and two paraphrases of its text, paraphrased skills A and B, each at temperature 0. A paraphrase rewrites the wording of the eight blocks and leaves the classification rules, the six-grade scale and the required output fields unchanged. Both are released with the analysis code (Supplementary Note 3). Extended Data Fig. 5 shows the absolute agreement of the skill and of each variation, with a percentile bootstrap over patients. Fig. 4e shows the stability of the reading on the 186 charts hepatologist B read. For each variation it gives the κ between the classification under that variation and under the skill at temperature 0, beside the same statistic for hepatologist A against hepatologist B and for MedDx against each of them, each with a percentile bootstrap interval over patients. The paired change from the skill at temperature 0 uses a paired bootstrap over the charts both conditions placed.

Quotation check

Each classification carries a supporting quotation that MedDx drew from the notes. Each quotation was compared with the notes MedDx read, ignoring differences in letter case, punctuation and spacing. A quotation is verbatim when it matches one passage of the notes word for word, or when it joins non-adjacent passages with an ellipsis and every passage matches. The remaining quotations are not verbatim and were separated by whether every word occurs in the patient's notes. The check shows that a quotation is in the notes, not that it supports the classification. Where MedDx returned an explanatory statement in place of a quotation, the classification is reported separately.

Note types or sections and rule findings.

The harness returns two further fields with every classification. The rule findings are the enumerated list the skill defines (Supplementary Note 3, section 3.1), and a classification returning none of them is recorded as having no rule finding. The note type or section is MedDx's own free-text report of where in the notes its evidence lay. The skill gives examples and leaves the set open. The figures show the ten most cited (Fig. 4g; Extended Data Fig. 6b) and the eight most cited among ground-truth-positive patients (Extended Data Fig. 6a). A classification may cite more than one note type or section, so citations exceed classifications, and in Extended Data Fig. 6a a patient is counted in every pair of rule finding and note type or section its classification carries. A classification carrying none of the four rule findings is drawn as other evidence, outside the rules. The third rule finding, a positive AUDIT or CAGE score, was carried by no patient. The 62 ground-truthindeterminate patients are omitted from these panels.

Statistical analysis

Unless stated otherwise, the following apply. Bootstrap confidence intervals used the percentile method with 2,000 resamples. The chart-scale patient-group differences and the agreement definitions used 4,000 resamples, and the treatment shares used 500 paired resamples. Intervals for binomial proportions are Wilson intervals 52 , and the share of quotations verbatim in the chart has a Clopper-Pearson exact interval 53 . Registry-scale quantities carry percentile bootstrap intervals. Estimated counts, shares and differences are each rounded from the unrounded value, so printed components can differ from a printed total by one in the last digit. The symmetry of the disagreements in Fig. 1c was tested with Bowker's test. Paired comparisons of correctness used the discordant pairs only 54 : an exact binomial test where there are fewer than 25 of them, and a continuitycorrected McNemar test otherwise. All analyses were conducted in Python 3.12.

MedDx read the notes of every patient in the study cohort and every one received hepatologist chart review. The charts were sampled from an earlier version of the registry data than the one analyzed, and patient record linkage was revised between the two. Record linkage to the registry was lost for 14 of the 499 sampled patients, who are not analyzed. Of the 243 patients sampled as having an AUD diagnosis, 231 carry one in the structured record analyzed, as do 2 of the 242 sampled as having none (233 in all); that record defines the AUD diagnosis in every analysis. The weighted sum is the registry estimate, 6,223 patients with AUD, which is the denominator for the treatment shares; the medication records supply the numerator. Hospital type is a weighting cell, whereas the hospital system that wrote a patient's notes is the variable on which the classifiers are compared (Fig. 3c; Supplementary Table 7).

Supplementary Note 3. The skill and its extraction schema

The eight blocks are reproduced below verbatim, as the text every engine was given. The worked examples in its section 7 are mock examples written for the skill and contain no text from any patient's chart. They are released as a standalone Agent Skill under CC-BY 4.0 (Code availability); the two paraphrased skills used as skill variations (Extended Data Fig. 5) are provided with the analysis code. The schema requires the engine to return, for every classification, the rule findings it identified (the skill's triggers), the note type or section supporting each, a verbatim quotation from the source note, and the classification rule it applied. Where the engine judged that the record held nothing about alcohol it returned that statement in the field instead of quoted text, and those classifications are reported separately (Fig. 4f).

--name: aud-adjudication description: > Classify Alcohol Use Disorder (AUD) status from clinical chart notes using a six-point ordinal scale. Designed for LLM-based chart review that replicates expert hepatology fellow adjudication. Applicable to any EHR note corpus where AUD status needs structured extraction. version: "1.0" license: CC-BY-4.0 ---# AUD Chart Adjudication -Guidance for the LLM Reviewer This is a self-contained instruction set for an LLM (or a new human reviewer) classifying whether a patient has Alcohol Use Disorder (AUD) from their clin ical chart. It is written to be followed literally and in order. -Definitely Yes: An explicit AUD diagnosis, documented alcohol dependence or abuse, alcoholic cirrhosis (the only alcohol-attributed disease that qualifi es on its own), a positive AUDIT (>=8) or CAGE (>=2), OR documented alcohol w ithdrawal and its management (including CIWA monitoring). Any ONE of these is enough.

-Probably Yes: No standalone trigger -BUT the chart documents heavy/extensi ve prior drinking, problematic patterns, OR alcohol-attributed sequelae OTHER than cirrhosis (alcoholic hepatitis, alcoholic pancreatitis, ALD, Wernicke/K orsakoff). These rise to Definitely Yes only when combined with a corroborati ng trigger.

-Uncertain: Alcohol use is mentioned, but quantity, pattern, or severity can not be determined (e.g., "Alcohol: Yes" or "drinks daily" with no further det ail).

-Probably No: Alcohol is mentioned only in the context of clearly social or occasional consumption.

-Definitely No: Documentation explicitly indicates the patient is a non-drin ker, OR alcohol is explicitly ruled out as a contributor (e.g., "no history o f ETOH abuse," "cirrhosis not due to alcohol").

-Not Enough Data: No mention of alcohol use anywhere in the reviewed chart. When structured fields (e.g., Social History box "Alcohol: No") and provider narrative (HPI, Assessment/Plan, progress notes) disagree, the narrative win s. Structured fields are often auto-populated or carried forward unchanged. A structured "Alcohol: No" next to narrative "history of alcoholic cirrhosis" = Definitely Yes.

Exception: if a structured field is the ONLY evidence and there is no narrati ve about alcohol at all, accept the structured field at face value. ## 4.2 Current sobriety never lowers the classification AUD is about whether the disorder exists or existed. "Sober since 2015," "in remission," "attends AA," and "quit drinking" are evidence that there was a d isorder to recover from. If Definitely Yes criteria are met historically, the answer stays Definitely Yes.

## 4.3 One trigger is enough

Do not wait for corroboration. A single Definitely Yes trigger settles the cl assification. ## 4.4 Definitely No includes "ruled out as a contributor" Use the broader definition: not only "patient is a non-drinker," but also any explicit statement ruling alcohol out -e.g., "no history of ETOH abuse," "c irrhosis not due to alcohol," "non-alcoholic steatohepatitis" when stated to exclude alcohol.

## 4.5 Classify only what is documented

Never infer a diagnosis the chart does not state. "Cirrhosis" with no stated cause is NOT automatically alcoholic. # 5. How to Handle Marginal Cases -Structured field conflicts with narrative → follow the narrative (Section 4.1).

-Single acute intoxication or "etoh fall" visit, no diagnosis/pattern/screen /withdrawal/sequelae → Probably Yes or Uncertain, NOT Definitely Yes. One eve nt is not a disorder.

-Alcohol-attributed sequelae OTHER than cirrhosis (alcoholic hepatitis, panc reatitis, ALD, Wernicke/Korsakoff) WITHOUT a corroborating trigger → Probably Yes. They rise to Definitely Yes only when paired with another trigger.

-"Alcohol: Yes" or "drinks daily" with no quantity/pattern/severity → Uncert ain.

-Clearly social/occasional use → Probably No. the three-class level and κ uses the primary definition, three classes with not enough data excluded, both on raw first-pass classifications before any adjudication. Data availability

The clinical notes and patient-level data used in this study contain protected health information and cannot be shared publicly. Summary statistics and de-identified analysis outputs are available in the Supplementary Information. Access to source data may be requested through the Indiana Network for Patient Care data governance process.

Code availability

The analysis code, from the hepatologists' classifications and the per-patient model classifications onward (the ground truth, the estimators, the registry estimate and the figure and table producers), is at https://github.com/Su-informatics-lab/aud. The skill is printed in full in Supplementary Note 3 and released under CC-BY 4.0 as a standalone Agent Skill in the same repository.

Competing interests

The authors declare no competing interests.

Author contributions

H.W. and J.S. conceived the study. H.W. developed MedDx, performed the analyses and wrote the manuscript. Y.C. verified the data and the analyses. C.X. and H.L. contributed to the formal analysis. D.P. managed the registry data. M.A., A.P.D. and S.G. reviewed the charts and contributed to the clinical interpretation. S.L. contributed to the clinical interpretation. W.T. supervised the statistical analysis. J.S. supervised the study.

Ethics

The Institutional Review Board of Indiana University waived ethical approval for this work (exempt determination, protocol 29101, exemption category 4(iii)). As secondary research on existing health records, it did not require informed consent.

Supplementary Information

Supplementary Note 1. Second readings, sampling strata and the estimates in Fig. 1 A chart had a single reading only when MedDx agreed with hepatologist A under each of the three engines (Methods). Of the 423 charts with an AUD+ or AUD-ground truth, 365 had a single reading, and all 15 of MedDx's errors and abstentions are among the 58 with a second reading. Of the 16 charts in which one of MedDx and hepatologist A was right and the other wrong, MedDx was correct in 10 (exact binomial P = 0.45; Fig. 1e). MedDx classified as AUD+ or AUD-98.1% of the 423 patients with an AUD+ or AUD-ground truth, compared with 95.3% for hepatologist A.

The study cohort was drawn in balanced numbers on the AUD diagnosis, so charts with the diagnosis are over-represented relative to the registry. Among charts with an AUD diagnosis in the structured record the diagnosis identifies every ground-truth-positive patient, and among charts without one it identifies none, so its sensitivity and specificity depend on the mix of the two groups. The absence of a code is scored as a negative classification throughout. Weighted to the registry, the specificity of the AUD diagnosis is 96% (Fig. 1d). MedDx's sensitivity is 99% among charts with the diagnosis (200 of 202) and 96% among charts without it (46 of 48), so weighting changes it little.

Supplementary Note 2. The alcohol-associated cirrhosis finding and the other alcohol-related diagnosis stratum

The ground truth treats alcohol-associated cirrhosis named as such in the notes as sufficient for AUD+, and the K70 codes that place a patient in the other alcohol-related diagnosis stratum record the same condition in the structured record, so the two can agree without either adding information about alcohol use. The hepatologists' review forms record a grade, a quotation and screening scores but not which finding decided the grade, so this check uses the rule findings MedDx reported for the same charts. On 29 of the 423 AUD+ or AUD-charts alcohol-associated cirrhosis was the only rule finding MedDx reported, 28 of them AUD+ in the ground truth; the 29 are 9 in the AUD diagnosis stratum, 8 in the other alcohol-related diagnosis stratum and 12 in the no alcohol-related diagnosis stratum.

Setting those 29 charts to indeterminate, so that they carry no weight, and leaving every other chart as it is gives a registry estimate of 6,044 patients with AUD (95% CI 5,518-6,559) against 6,223: 2,424 (92.3%) in the AUD diagnosis stratum, 1,129 (71.0%) in the other alcohol-related diagnosis stratum and 2,491 (24.5%) in the no alcohol-related diagnosis stratum. The shares on an AUD-specific drug by five years become 10.8% (9.2-12.6), 5.2% (3.5-7.9) and 3.1% (2.3-4.3), against 10.8%, 4.6% and 3.1%. # 1.

What You Are Being Asked to DoYou will be given the clinical chart documentation for one patient at a time. Your single job is to read the chart and assign exactly one label from a six -point scale describing how confident the evidence is that this patient has A lcohol Use Disorder. You are not diagnosing the patient. You are reporting what the documentation supports. Think of yourself as a careful reader summarizing what the chart sa ys about alcohol -not a doctor deciding what is true. Three things must accompany every classification: (a) which evidence categori es supported your decision, (b) any screening-tool scores you found (AUDIT/CA GE), and (c) a short supporting quote with where it came from (HPI, problem l ist, social history, etc.). Never give a label without the quote that justifi es it. # 2. Read This Before You Start -Your task is to replicate what a careful chart reviewer would conclude. Fol low the rules precisely, not your own clinical intuition. -The review window matters. Focus on documentation most relevant to the clin ical question. Evidence far outside the period of interest is context, not pr imary basis. -One patient may have many documents. Read all of them for that patient befo re deciding. Evidence in any one note counts for the whole patient. # 3. The Six-Point Scale Assign exactly one:

How these collapse for analysis: Definitely Yes + Probably Yes = "AUD Presen t." Definitely No + Probably No = "AUD Absent." Uncertain and Not Enough Data are handled separately. ## 3.1 The Definitely Yes Triggers -Memorize These Any ONE of the following locks the classification at Definitely Yes. Nothing later in the chart can downgrade it. 1. Alcoholic cirrhosis named as such -Cirrhosis is the ONLY alcohol-attribut ed disease that is a standalone trigger. 2. Explicit AUD diagnosis or "alcohol dependence / alcohol abuse / alcoholism " in the narrative -Direct evidence. 3. Positive screening tool: AUDIT >=8 or CAGE >=2 -Validated instruments cro ssing their clinical threshold. 4. Documented alcohol withdrawal OR its management -including CIWA protocol, benzodiazepine taper for withdrawal, DTs, detox. CIWA placement counts EVEN IF the patient never manifests withdrawal signs. # 4. The Principles You Must Follow ## 4.1 Narrative beats structured fields (most important rule)

-

Conflicting frequency entries in structured fields → let the clinical pictu re in the narrative decide. -Patient sober/in remission but historical triggers present → Definitely Yes (Section 4.2). -No mention of alcohol anywhere → Not Enough Data. # 6. Common Mistakes and Corrections -Trusting structured "Alcohol: No" over narrative → Narrative wins. Alcoholi c cirrhosis in HPI = Definitely Yes regardless of structured field. -Downgrading because patient is sober → Current abstinence is irrelevant. So ber 10 years + alcoholic cirrhosis = still Definitely Yes. -Requiring several triggers → Any ONE trigger is sufficient. -CIWA only counting if withdrawal occurred → CIWA initiation alone is the tr igger. -"Cirrhosis not due to alcohol" classified as Not Enough Data → This is Defi nitely No. -Single intoxication = Definitely Yes → One acute event without diagnosis/pa ttern/withdrawal is Probably Yes or Uncertain. -Inferring unstated diagnoses → Classify only what is documented. No alcohol mention = Not Enough Data. # 7. Worked Examples Example 1 -Structured-field trap: Social History "Alcohol: No" but HPI says "hx of alcohol related cirrhosis with portal HTN" → Definitely Yes. Narrative wins. Example 2 -Clean denial: "Alcohol use: Denies." No alcohol-related anything → Definitely No. Example 3 -CIWA without withdrawal: Patient on CIWA protocol, no formal AUD diagnosis → Definitely Yes. CIWA initiation is a standalone trigger. Example 4 -Single intoxication: ER note for etoh fall, one event, no diagnos is/pattern → Probably Yes or Uncertain, NOT Definitely Yes. Example 5 -Sober for years: "Alcoholic cirrhosis" + "sober since 2014, atten ds AA" → Definitely Yes. Sobriety never lowers. Example 6 -Alcohol ruled out: "NASH cirrhosis. No history of ETOH abuse." → Definitely No. Example 7 -Non-cirrhosis sequela alone: "Alcoholic pancreatitis" with no oth er triggers → Probably Yes (only cirrhosis is a standalone trigger). Example 8 -Sequela + corroboration: "Alcoholic hepatitis" + "CIWA initiated" → Definitely Yes. CIWA is a standalone trigger. # 8. Final Checklist Before submitting each classification: -Did I read every document for this patient? -Did I check whether any single Definitely Yes trigger is present? -If structured and narrative disagree, did I follow the narrative? -Did I avoid downgrading because the patient is currently sober? -Is my label backed by an actual quote from the chart, with its source locat ion? -Did I record any AUDIT/CAGE scores and the evidence categories I used? -Am I classifying only what is documented -not what I assume?
